

Q: Who discovered the sea route from Europe to India in 1498?
CLASS: Competitive
SUBJECT: Modern History
CHAPTER: Arrival of Europeans in India
LEVEL: Easy
A: Christopher Columbus
B: Vasco da Gama
C: Ferdinand Magellan
D: Marco Polo
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in 1498 by sailing around the Cape of Good Hope.

Q: In which year did Vasco da Gama reach Calicut?
CLASS: Competitive
SUBJECT: Modern History
CHAPTER: Arrival of Europeans in India
LEVEL: Easy
A: 1492
B: 1494
C: 1498
D: 1505
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Vasco da Gama reached Calicut on the Malabar Coast in 1498.

Q: Which ruler was associated with Calicut when Vasco da Gama arrived in India?
CLASS: Competitive
SUBJECT: Modern History
CHAPTER: Arrival of Europeans in India
LEVEL: Easy
A: The Zamorin
B: The Nizam
C: The Nawab of Bengal
D: The Peshwa
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The Zamorin was the ruler of Calicut when Vasco da Gama arrived.

Q: Which cape did Vasco da Gama sail around on his voyage to India?
CLASS: Competitive
SUBJECT: Modern History
CHAPTER: Arrival of Europeans in India
LEVEL: Easy
A: Cape Horn
B: Cape Verde
C: Cape Comorin
D: Cape of Good Hope
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: Vasco da Gama reached India by sailing around the Cape of Good Hope at the southern tip of Africa.

Q: Which European power introduced the cartaz system in the Indian Ocean?
CLASS: Competitive
SUBJECT: Modern History
CHAPTER: Arrival of Europeans in India
LEVEL: Tough
A: Portuguese
B: Dutch
C: English
D: French
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The Portuguese used the cartaz system, a naval pass system intended to control maritime trade.

Q: The cartaz system was primarily related to which activity?
CLASS: Competitive
SUBJECT: Modern History
CHAPTER: Arrival of Europeans in India
LEVEL: Moderate
A: Land revenue collection
B: Village administration
C: Religious education
D: Control and licensing of maritime trade
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The cartaz system required many merchant vessels to obtain Portuguese passes to trade or sail in controlled waters.

Q1. [Easy] Which event encouraged Europeans to search for a direct sea route to India?
A. The fall of Constantinople to the Ottoman Turks in 1453
B. The discovery of Australia
C. The Industrial Revolution in India
D. The invention of the railway
ANSWER: A. The fall of Constantinople to the Ottoman Turks in 1453
EXPLANATION: In 1453, Constantinople fell to the Ottoman Turks, making existing trade routes more difficult for Europeans.

Q2. [Easy] Which goods from India were in great demand in Europe?
A. Coal and iron only
B. Spices, calicoes, silk and precious stones
C. Petroleum and machines
D. Tea and coffee only
ANSWER: B. Spices, calicoes, silk and precious stones
EXPLANATION: Indian spices, textiles, silk and precious stones were highly valued in European markets.

Q3. [Easy] Which European country pioneered the sea route to India?
A. Denmark
B. France
C. Portugal
D. The Netherlands
ANSWER: C. Portugal
EXPLANATION: The Portuguese were the pioneers among the Europeans who came to India by sea.

Q4. [Easy] Who reached Calicut in May 1498?
A. Vasco da Gama
B. Francisco de Almeida
C. Alfonso de Albuquerque
D. Pedro Cabral
ANSWER: A. Vasco da Gama
EXPLANATION: Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in May 1498 with a Gujarati pilot named Abdul Majid.

Q5. [Easy] Who was the ruler of Calicut when Vasco da Gama arrived?
A. Nizam of Hyderabad
B. Zamorin
C. Sultan of Bijapur
D. Mughal emperor
ANSWER: B. Zamorin
EXPLANATION: The Hindu ruler of Calicut was known as the Zamorin or Samuthiri.

Q6. [Easy] What was the name of the Portuguese policy associated with Francisco de Almeida?
A. Subsidiary Alliance
B. Doctrine of Lapse
C. Blue Water Policy
D. Permanent Settlement
ANSWER: C. Blue Water Policy
EXPLANATION: Almeida's policy focused on naval power and is known as the Blue Water Policy or cartaze system.

Q7. [Easy] Who captured Goa from the Sultan of Bijapur in 1510?
A. Vasco da Gama
B. Alfonso de Albuquerque
C. Nino da Cunha
D. Francisco de Almeida
ANSWER: B. Alfonso de Albuquerque
EXPLANATION: Albuquerque captured Goa in 1510 and became a key founder of Portuguese power in the East.

Q8. [Easy] Who shifted the Portuguese headquarters in India from Cochin to Goa?
A. Nino da Cunha
B. Robert Clive
C. Dupleix
D. Thomas Roe
ANSWER: A. Nino da Cunha
EXPLANATION: Nino da Cunha shifted the Portuguese headquarters from Cochin to Goa around 1530.

Q9. [Easy] Which European company was formed in the Netherlands in 1602?
A. French East India Company
B. Danish East India Company
C. Dutch East India Company
D. English East India Company
ANSWER: C. Dutch East India Company
EXPLANATION: The Dutch East India Company was established in 1602.

Q10. [Easy] Where did the Dutch establish their first factory in India in 1605?
A. Surat
B. Masulipatnam
C. Calcutta
D. Pondicherry
ANSWER: B. Masulipatnam
EXPLANATION: The Dutch founded their first Indian factory at Masulipatnam in 1605.

Q11. [Easy] In which year was the English East India Company chartered by Queen Elizabeth I?
A. 1498
B. 1530
C. 1600
D. 1664
ANSWER: C. 1600
EXPLANATION: Queen Elizabeth I issued the charter on 31 December 1600.

Q12. [Easy] Which English queen issued the charter establishing the East India Company in 1600?
A. Queen Elizabeth I
B. Queen Victoria
C. Queen Mary I
D. Queen Anne
ANSWER: A. Queen Elizabeth I
EXPLANATION: Queen Elizabeth I issued the charter on 31 December 1600.

Q13. [Easy] Which English sailor arrived at Jahangir's court in 1609?
A. Captain Thomas Best
B. William Hawkins
C. Francis Day
D. Robert Clive
ANSWER: B. William Hawkins
EXPLANATION: William Hawkins reached Jahangir's court in 1609.

Q14. [Easy] Which English settlement became the headquarters on the east coast after Masulipatnam?
A. Madras
B. Surat
C. Bombay
D. Patna
ANSWER: A. Madras
EXPLANATION: Madras, with Fort St. George, replaced Masulipatnam as the English headquarters in south India.

Q15. [Easy] Which three villages formed the basis of Calcutta?
A. Agra, Delhi and Lahore
B. Sutanuti, Gobindapur and Kalikata

D. Surat, Broach and Ahmedabad

Q16. [Easy] What was the name of the French East India Company?

Q31. [Easy] Who issued the famous farmans in 1717 to the English East India Company?

Q34. [Easy] Which city was gifted to Charles II as part of a Portuguese royal dowry?

Q35. [Easy] Which European power became the strongest by the end of the eighteenth century?

B. Denmark
C. England
D. France
ANSWER: C. England
EXPLANATION: England emerged as the most successful European power in India by the end of the eighteenth century.

Q36. [Moderate] Why did Europeans seek a direct sea route to India?
A. They wanted to avoid all ocean travel
B. Arab intermediaries and Ottoman control made existing trade routes costly and difficult
C. India had no trading ports
D. European rulers banned the spice trade
ANSWER: B. Arab intermediaries and Ottoman control made existing trade routes costly and difficult
EXPLANATION: Arab intermediaries controlled much of the trade and land routes, while Ottoman power restricted access.

Q37. [Moderate] What role did Prince Henry of Portugal play in exploration?
A. He led the Battle of Plassey
B. He promoted the search for an ocean route to India
C. He founded the French Company
D. He captured Calcutta
ANSWER: B. He promoted the search for an ocean route to India
EXPLANATION: Prince Henry, called the Navigator, strongly promoted maritime exploration and a route to India.

Q38. [Moderate] What did the Treaty of Tordesillas (1494) do?
A. Divided non-Christian overseas claims between Spain and Portugal
B. Ended the Carnatic Wars
C. Gave Goa to the English
D. Created the Dutch East India Company
ANSWER: A. Divided non-Christian overseas claims between Spain and Portugal
EXPLANATION: The treaty used an imaginary Atlantic line to divide overseas claims between Spain and Portugal.

Q39. [Moderate] Who rounded the Cape of Good Hope in 1487?
A. Vasco da Gama
B. Bartholomew Dias
C. Pedro Cabral
D. Abdul Majid
ANSWER: B. Bartholomew Dias
EXPLANATION: Bartholomew Dias rounded the Cape of Good Hope in 1487.

Q40. [Moderate] Why was Vasco da Gama's voyage commercially important to Portugal?
A. It ended all trade with Asia
B. It gave direct access to the profitable spice trade
C. It discovered coal in India
D. It established British rule
ANSWER: B. It gave direct access to the profitable spice trade
EXPLANATION: Direct access to pepper and other goods enabled Portuguese merchants to make large profits.

Q41. [Moderate] Why did the Zamorin initially welcome Vasco da Gama?
A. He wanted to make Calicut a trading centre and saw no immediate threat
B. He had already surrendered to Portugal
C. He wanted to attack the Mughal Empire
D. He was allied with England against France
ANSWER: A. He wanted to make Calicut a trading centre and saw no immediate threat
EXPLANATION: Calicut prospered as an entrepot, so the Zamorin initially welcomed foreign traders.

Q42. [Moderate] What was the main aim of the Portuguese in the Indian Ocean trade?
A. To share power equally with all traders
B. To monopolise eastern trade and exclude competitors
C. To end maritime trade
D. To promote Dutch settlements
ANSWER: B. To monopolise eastern trade and exclude competitors
EXPLANATION: The Portuguese sought to monopolise trade, especially by excluding Arab competitors.

Q43. [Moderate] What was Almeida's main strategic idea?
A. Control the Indian Ocean through naval strength
B. Build a capital at Calcutta
C. Capture all inland farming villages
D. Avoid building forts
ANSWER: A. Control the Indian Ocean through naval strength
EXPLANATION: Almeida believed control at sea was more important than forts on land.

Q44. [Moderate] What was the cartaze system associated with the Portuguese?
A. A permit system for ships
B. A land revenue system
C. A system of village elections
D. A method of textile weaving
ANSWER: A. A permit system for ships
EXPLANATION: The Portuguese used naval power and a permit system to control shipping.

Q45. [Moderate] Which territory did Albuquerque acquire in 1510?
A. Goa
B. Madras
C. Pondicherry
D. Calcutta
ANSWER: A. Goa
EXPLANATION: Albuquerque captured Goa from the Sultan of Bijapur in 1510.

Q46. [Moderate] Which policy did Albuquerque use to encourage a permanent Portuguese population in India?
A. Encouraged Portuguese men to marry local women
B. Banned all settlement
C. Moved the capital to Surat
D. Ended trade with Indian merchants
ANSWER: A. Encouraged Portuguese men to marry local women
EXPLANATION: Albuquerque encouraged Portuguese men who stayed in India to marry local women.

Q47. [Moderate] Who shifted the Portuguese headquarters to Goa and expanded influence in Bengal?
A. Nino da Cunha
B. Francisco de Almeida
C. Dupleix
D. Thomas Best
ANSWER: A. Nino da Cunha
EXPLANATION: Nino da Cunha shifted the headquarters to Goa and attempted to increase Portuguese influence in Bengal.

Q48. [Moderate] Why did the Portuguese have an early advantage over many Indian powers?
A. They possessed organised naval forces and ship-mounted cannons
B. They controlled all Indian land armies
C. They had no European rivals
D. They avoided the sea
ANSWER: A. They possessed organised naval forces and ship-mounted cannons
EXPLANATION: Portuguese naval organisation and cannon-equipped ships gave them an advantage.

Q49. [Moderate] Why did Portuguese power decline in India?
A. They had no trading settlements
B. Corruption, piracy, religious persecution and stronger English and Dutch competition
C. They abandoned all sea travel in 1498
D. They never controlled any ports
ANSWER: B. Corruption, piracy, religious persecution and stronger English and Dutch competition
EXPLANATION: Several political, religious, commercial and military factors weakened Portuguese power.

Q50. [Moderate] Which event led to the Portuguese loss of Hooghly in 1632?
A. Shah Jahan ordered action after Portuguese abuses including slave trading
B. The Dutch gifted it to the Portuguese
C. The English abandoned Bengal
D. The Zamorin requested it
ANSWER: A. Shah Jahan ordered action after Portuguese abuses including slave trading
EXPLANATION: Shah Jahan ordered the Bengal governor to act against the Portuguese, and the Mughal siege captured Hooghly.

Q51. [Moderate] What was the Dutch company's main commercial focus?
A. The Spice Islands and trade in spices
B. Building an empire in North India
C. Converting the Mughal court
D. Controlling the silk road by land
ANSWER: A. The Spice Islands and trade in spices
EXPLANATION: The Dutch prioritised profitable trade in Indonesia and the Spice Islands.

Q52. [Moderate] What happened at Amboyna in 1623?
A. The Dutch massacred Englishmen and Japanese
B. The English defeated the French
C. The Portuguese captured Goa
D. The Danes sold Tranquebar
ANSWER: A. The Dutch massacred Englishmen and Japanese
EXPLANATION: The Amboyna incident intensified Anglo-Dutch rivalry after the Dutch killed ten Englishmen and nine Japanese.

Q53. [Moderate] Why did the Dutch withdraw from India after the 1667 compromise?
A. They chose to focus on more profitable trade in Indonesia
B. They were given Calcutta by the Mughals
C. They became allies of the French in India
D. They stopped trading in spices
ANSWER: A. They chose to focus on more profitable trade in Indonesia
EXPLANATION: The Dutch concentrated on Indonesia, where their spice trade was more profitable.

Q54. [Moderate] What was the immediate outcome of Captain Thomas Best's naval success in 1612?
A. The English gained permission to establish a factory at Surat
B. The French captured Pondicherry
C. The Portuguese acquired Bengal
D. The Dutch abandoned Indonesia
ANSWER: A. The English gained permission to establish a factory at Surat
EXPLANATION: Thomas Best's victory over the Portuguese helped the English obtain permission for a Surat factory.

Q55. [Moderate] Who was the English ambassador who visited Jahangir's court from 1615?
A. Sir Thomas Roe
B. William Hedges
C. Francis Day
D. John Surman
ANSWER: A. Sir Thomas Roe
EXPLANATION: Sir Thomas Roe visited Jahangir as the accredited ambassador of James I.

Q56. [Moderate] How did the English obtain Bombay for their Company?
A. It was leased by Charles II to the Company for an annual payment
B. It was conquered by Robert Clive
C. It was granted by Farrukhsiyar in 1717
D. It was purchased from the Dutch in 1759
ANSWER: A. It was leased by Charles II to the Company for an annual payment
EXPLANATION: Charles II received Bombay as dowry and leased it to the East India Company in 1668.

Q57. [Moderate] What was the Golden Farman of 1632?
A. A grant from the Sultan of Golconda allowing English trade in its ports for an annual payment
B. A French order to fortify Pondicherry
C. A Portuguese permit for all ships
D. A Danish treaty with Bengal
ANSWER: A. A grant from the Sultan of Golconda allowing English trade in its ports for an annual payment
EXPLANATION: The Golden Farman allowed the English to trade freely in Golconda's ports for 500 pagodas a year.

Q58. [Moderate] Who gave the English permission to build a fortified factory at Madras in 1639?
A. The ruler of Chandragiri
B. The Sultan of Bijapur
C. The Zamorin
D. The Nawab of Bengal
ANSWER: A. The ruler of Chandragiri
EXPLANATION: Francis Day received permission from the ruler of Chandragiri to build a fortified factory at Madras.

Q59. [Moderate] What annual payment allowed the English to trade in Bengal under Shah Shuja's 1651 permission?
A. Rs 300
B. Rs 3,000
C. Rs 30,000
D. 10,000 pagodas
ANSWER: B. Rs 3,000
EXPLANATION: Shah Shuja permitted English trade in Bengal for Rs 3,000 a year in lieu of duties.

Q60. [Moderate] Who negotiated the English return to Sutanuti in 1690?
A. Job Charnock
B. Robert Clive
C. Eyre Coote
D. Dupleix
ANSWER: A. Job Charnock
EXPLANATION: Job Charnock negotiated with the Mughals and returned to Sutanuti in 1690.

Q61. [Moderate] Which three villages formed the English settlement that developed into Calcutta?
A. Sutanuti, Gobindapur and Kalikata
B. Masulipatnam, Pulicat and Surat
C. Cochin, Calicut and Cannanore
D. Mahe, Karaikal and Balasore
ANSWER: A. Sutanuti, Gobindapur and Kalikata
EXPLANATION: The English acquired these three villages and later fortified the settlement as Fort William.

Q62. [Moderate] What privileges did Farrukhsiyar's farmans provide?
A. Trading concessions, dastaks and permission to rent more land around Calcutta
B. A right to rule all of India
C. A ban on all Indian merchants
D. Ownership of every port in the Mughal Empire
ANSWER: A. Trading concessions, dastaks and permission to rent more land around Calcutta
EXPLANATION: The farmans gave the Company customs concessions, passes for goods and land-rental privileges.

Q63. [Moderate] Who founded the French factory at Surat in 1667?
A. Francois Caron
B. Francois Martin
C. Dupleix
D. Count de Lally
ANSWER: A. Francois Caron
EXPLANATION: Francois Caron led the French expedition that established a factory at Surat.

Q64. [Moderate] Who was the French governor associated with Pondicherry's development?
A. Francois Martin
B. La Bourdonnais
C. Bussy
D. D'Ache
ANSWER: A. Francois Martin
EXPLANATION: Francois Martin developed Pondicherry as the French stronghold in India.

Q65. [Moderate] Why did the Dutch capture Pondicherry in 1693?
A. They were at war with France and allied with the English
B. They were helping the Portuguese
C. They were invited by the Mughal emperor
D. They wanted to support Dupleix
ANSWER: A. They were at war with France and allied with the English
EXPLANATION: The Dutch captured Pondicherry during conflict with France, aided by their alliance with England.

Q66. [Moderate] What was the main result of the Second Carnatic War?
A. It strengthened French power permanently
B. It was inconclusive but weakened French influence relative to the English
C. It ended all European trade
D. It gave Madras to Portugal
ANSWER: B. It was inconclusive but weakened French influence relative to the English
EXPLANATION: The Second Carnatic War was inconclusive but undermined French power in South India.

Q67. [Moderate] What was the immediate trigger of the First Carnatic War in India?
A. The French seized Madras in 1746 after English naval actions
B. The Dutch captured Goa
C. The Danes attacked Calcutta
D. The English captured Pondicherry in 1600
ANSWER: A. The French seized Madras in 1746 after English naval actions
EXPLANATION: After English naval actions against French ships, the French seized Madras in 1746.

Q68. [Moderate] What did the Battle of St. Thome demonstrate to Europeans?
A. A small disciplined army could defeat a much larger army
B. Naval power was no longer useful
C. The French could not fight on land
D. Indian armies never used cavalry
ANSWER: A. A small disciplined army could defeat a much larger army
EXPLANATION: A small French force defeated a larger Indian force, showing the importance of discipline and military organisation.

Q69. [Moderate] Which factors helped the English defeat the French in India?
A. Stronger navy, better finances, important bases and capable commanders
B. A complete lack of trade
C. Support from the Portuguese alone
D. French control of Calcutta and Bombay
ANSWER: A. Stronger navy, better finances, important bases and capable commanders
EXPLANATION: The English had naval, financial, geographic and leadership advantages.

Q70. [Tough] Why did the Portuguese initially gain influence despite being numerically limited?
A. Their naval unity, cannon-equipped ships and control of strategic ports gave them leverage
B. They had the largest land army in India
C. All Indian rulers supported their monopoly
D. They controlled every inland trade route
ANSWER: A. Their naval unity, cannon-equipped ships and control of strategic ports gave them leverage
EXPLANATION: Portuguese naval organisation and strategic bases helped them influence maritime trade and coastal rulers.

Q71. [Tough] Which sequence correctly shows the early European arrivals in India as summarised in the PDF?
A. Portuguese, Dutch, English, Danes, French
B. English, French, Dutch, Portuguese, Danes
C. French, Danes, Portuguese, English, Dutch
D. Dutch, Portuguese, French, Danes, English
ANSWER: A. Portuguese, Dutch, English, Danes, French
EXPLANATION: The PDF's summary identifies the Portuguese as pioneers, followed by the Dutch, English, Danes and French.

Q72. [Tough] Which pairing is correct?
A. Almeida—Blue Water Policy; Albuquerque—capture of Goa
B. Almeida—founding Pondicherry; Albuquerque—Battle of Wandiwash
C. Nino da Cunha—Battle of Bidara; Dupleix—capture of Goa
D. Thomas Roe—capture of Hooghly; Caron—capture of Bombay
ANSWER: A. Almeida—Blue Water Policy; Albuquerque—capture of Goa
EXPLANATION: Almeida is associated with the Blue Water Policy, while Albuquerque captured Goa in 1510.

Q73. [Tough] Which sequence of Portuguese developments is correct?
A. Vasco da Gama arrives (1498) → Albuquerque captures Goa (1510) → Goa becomes headquarters (1530)
B. Goa becomes headquarters (1498) → Vasco arrives (1510) → Albuquerque captures Calcutta (1530)
C. Albuquerque captures Goa (1498) → Vasco arrives (1530) → Diu captured (1600)
D. Portuguese leave Goa (1510) → found Calcutta (1530) → arrive at Calicut (1663)
ANSWER: A. Vasco da Gama arrives (1498) → Albuquerque captures Goa (1510) → Goa becomes headquarters (1530)
EXPLANATION: The PDF gives Vasco da Gama's arrival in 1498, Goa's capture in 1510, and the shift of headquarters to Goa in 1530.

Q74. [Tough] Why was the Portuguese capture of Goa historically significant?
A. It became an important European territorial foothold in India
B. It ended all Portuguese activity in Asia
C. It immediately made Portugal ruler of all India
D. It created the English East India Company
ANSWER: A. It became an important European territorial foothold in India
EXPLANATION: Goa became a major Portuguese base and an early European territorial possession in India.

Q75. [Tough] Which event best explains why the English were impressed by their own naval strength in Jahangir's time?
A. The English victory over a Portuguese fleet in 1612
B. The French capture of Madras in 1746
C. The Dutch capture of Pondicherry in 1693
D. The Danish sale of Serampore in 1845
ANSWER: A. The English victory over a Portuguese fleet in 1612
EXPLANATION: Thomas Best's success against the Portuguese in 1612 impressed Jahangir and helped the English gain trading privileges.

Q76. [Tough] Why did Portuguese relations with the Mughal court deteriorate?
A. Piracy, capturing Mughal ships and imprisoning Muslims angered Jahangir and later rulers
B. The Portuguese refused to trade spices
C. The English asked them to rule Bengal
D. The Portuguese surrendered Goa to Jahangir
ANSWER: A. Piracy, capturing Mughal ships and imprisoning Muslims angered Jahangir and later rulers
EXPLANATION: Portuguese piracy and attacks on Mughal shipping provoked the Mughal authorities.

Q77. [Tough] Which statement about the English and Dutch compromise of 1667 is correct?
A. The English withdrew claims in Indonesia while the Dutch concentrated on Indonesia and reduced their Indian involvement
B. The Dutch handed all Indonesia to the English
C. The English withdrew from all of India
D. Both companies merged into one Dutch company
ANSWER: A. The English withdrew claims in Indonesia while the Dutch concentrated on Indonesia and reduced their Indian involvement
EXPLANATION: The compromise reduced Anglo-Dutch conflict: the English gave up claims in Indonesia and the Dutch focused there.

Q78. [Tough] What did the defeat of the Dutch at Bidara in 1759 signify?
A. A major blow to Dutch ambitions in India
B. The beginning of French rule over Bengal
C. The Portuguese reconquest of Goa
D. The establishment of Danish rule in Madras
ANSWER: A. A major blow to Dutch ambitions in India
EXPLANATION: The English victory at Bidara weakened Dutch political and military ambitions in India.

Q79. [Tough] Which event is correctly paired with its result?
A. Treaty of Paris (1763)—French settlements retained for commerce, but fortification was restricted
B. Treaty of Aix-La-Chapelle—French received Calcutta permanently
C. Treaty of Ryswick—English received Pondicherry
D. Treaty of Tordesillas—Dutch gained Goa
ANSWER: A. Treaty of Paris (1763)—French settlements retained for commerce, but fortification was restricted
EXPLANATION: The Treaty of Paris restored French factories but restricted their political and military role.

Q80. [Tough] Why was the First Carnatic War important beyond its immediate territorial result?
A. It demonstrated the effectiveness of disciplined European troops and naval power
B. It immediately ended all European rivalries
C. It established Danish supremacy
D. It gave the French control over all Mughal territory
ANSWER: A. It demonstrated the effectiveness of disciplined European troops and naval power
EXPLANATION: The Battle of St. Thome showed that a small disciplined force could defeat a larger army, and the war highlighted naval power.

Q81. [Tough] Which description of the English East India Company best matches the PDF?
A. A private company with elected directors and influential shareholders
B. A department fully controlled by the French king
C. A Danish missionary organisation
D. A Portuguese royal navy unit
ANSWER: A. A private company with elected directors and influential shareholders
EXPLANATION: The English Company was a private enterprise whose directors were elected and shareholders had influence.

Q82. [Tough] Why was the French East India Company at a disadvantage compared with the English Company?
A. It was heavily controlled by the State and suffered from weak shareholder involvement and slow decisions
B. It had no settlements in India at all
C. It had a larger navy than Britain
D. It was prohibited from trading in textiles
ANSWER: A. It was heavily controlled by the State and suffered from weak shareholder involvement and slow decisions
EXPLANATION: The French Company was state-controlled and lacked the same commercial independence and incentives.

Q83. [Tough] Which was a major reason for British naval superiority?
A. The Royal Navy was large and technologically advanced, and the British improved their fleet
B. Britain refused to build ships
C. France provided all British ships
D. British success depended only on cavalry
ANSWER: A. The Royal Navy was large and technologically advanced, and the British improved their fleet
EXPLANATION: The PDF attributes British success partly to the size, speed and technological strength of the Royal Navy.

Q84. [Tough] How did the Industrial Revolution contribute to English success in India?
A. It increased production and helped England maintain an economic and technological advantage
B. It ended textile production in Britain
C. It made the English dependent on Dutch factories
D. It caused Britain to abandon overseas trade
ANSWER: A. It increased production and helped England maintain an economic and technological advantage
EXPLANATION: New machinery improved production and helped England retain an advantage over European rivals.

Q85. [Tough] What role did financial markets play in Britain's overseas expansion?
A. Government debt markets helped Britain raise funds to finance wars
B. They prevented the government from borrowing
C. They were used only to buy spices
D. They replaced the Royal Navy
ANSWER: A. Government debt markets helped Britain raise funds to finance wars
EXPLANATION: The Bank of England and debt markets helped Britain finance military expenditure on a scale rivals struggled to match.

Q86. [Tough] Which factor made British rule more acceptable to some subjects than Portuguese or Spanish rule, according to the PDF?
A. Britain was comparatively less zealous about spreading Christianity
B. Britain banned all local customs
C. Britain had no commercial interests
D. Britain avoided military force entirely
ANSWER: A. Britain was comparatively less zealous about spreading Christianity
EXPLANATION: The PDF says Britain was comparatively less interested in religious conversion than Portugal, Spain or the Dutch.

Q87. [Tough] Which statement about Indian goods in European trade is supported by the PDF?
A. Indian cotton textiles, silk, dyes and printed fabrics were traded in Europe and other parts of Asia
B. Indian goods were only consumed inside India
C. European companies traded only in machinery
D. Textiles were not important before Europeans arrived
ANSWER: A. Indian cotton textiles, silk, dyes and printed fabrics were traded in Europe and other parts of Asia
EXPLANATION: Indian textiles and silk were established exports and were traded across Europe and Asia.

Q88. [Tough] How were Indian textiles connected to wider global trade networks described in the PDF?
A. They could be exchanged in Southeast Asia for spices and were part of wider intercontinental trading circuits
B. They were never exported beyond Europe
C. They were used only to pay Indian soldiers
D. They replaced all African trade
ANSWER: A. They could be exchanged in Southeast Asia for spices and were part of wider intercontinental trading circuits
EXPLANATION: European traders carried Indian textiles to Asian markets and used them in exchange for spices and other goods.

Q89. [Tough] Which statement best describes the Danish presence in India?
A. It remained commercially limited, with Tranquebar and Serampore as key settlements
B. It conquered Bengal and ruled until 1858
C. It defeated the English at Wandiwash
D. It founded the first Portuguese factory
ANSWER: A. It remained commercially limited, with Tranquebar and Serampore as key settlements
EXPLANATION: The Danish factories were never very important and were sold to the British government in 1845.

Q90. [Tough] Which was the most direct political outcome of the three Carnatic Wars?
A. The English emerged as the dominant European power in India over the French
B. The French became rulers of all India
C. The Dutch gained permanent control of Madras
D. The Portuguese recovered all lost settlements
ANSWER: A. The English emerged as the dominant European power in India over the French
EXPLANATION: The Carnatic Wars decided the Anglo-French contest in favour of the English.

Q91. [Tough] Why is Wandiwash sometimes treated as a turning point alongside Plassey?
A. The English victory removed their major European rival in India
B. It ended Mughal rule in 1526
C. It gave the Dutch control of Bengal
D. It was the first arrival of Europeans at Calicut
ANSWER: A. The English victory removed their major European rival in India
EXPLANATION: Wandiwash decisively weakened the French, leaving the English East India Company without a major European rival in India.

Q92. [Easy] Which Mughal emperor received English envoy William Hawkins in 1609?
A. Akbar
B. Jahangir
C. Shah Jahan
D. Farrukhsiyar
ANSWER: B. Jahangir
EXPLANATION: Hawkins was received by Jahangir in 1609.

Q93. [Moderate] Which English settlement became the seat of the eastern presidency in 1700?
A. Fort William at Calcutta
B. Fort St. George at Madras
C. Bombay Castle
D. Fort St. David
ANSWER: A. Fort William at Calcutta
EXPLANATION: The fortified settlement named Fort William became the seat of the eastern presidency at Calcutta in 1700.

Q94. [Moderate] Which European company was especially associated with missionary activities in India rather than major commercial success?
A. Danish
B. Dutch
C. English
D. Portuguese only
ANSWER: A. Danish
EXPLANATION: The Danes are described in the PDF as better known for missionary work than commerce.

Q95. [Moderate] What did the English company obtain through the farmans of Farrukhsiyar in 1717?
A. Trading privileges in Bengal, Gujarat and Hyderabad
B. Complete sovereignty over India
C. Permission to expel all Indian merchants
D. A monopoly over all European shipping
ANSWER: A. Trading privileges in Bengal, Gujarat and Hyderabad
EXPLANATION: The farmans granted the Company several valuable trading concessions in these regions.

Q96. [Tough] Why did English trading factories gradually become political enclaves?
A. Commercial strongholds expanded under English control and were increasingly fortified
B. Indian merchants stopped producing goods
C. The French handed over all their territories
D. The English refused to establish settlements
ANSWER: A. Commercial strongholds expanded under English control and were increasingly fortified
EXPLANATION: The PDF explains that commercial outposts grew into stronger settlements and eventually helped the English consolidate political control.

Q97. [Tough] Which comparison between English and French bases is given as a factor in English success?
A. The English held Calcutta, Bombay and Madras, while the French mainly had Pondicherry
B. The French held Bombay, Madras and Calcutta
C. The English had only Pondicherry
D. Both sides controlled exactly the same ports
ANSWER: A. The English held Calcutta, Bombay and Madras, while the French mainly had Pondicherry
EXPLANATION: The English controlled three important centres, while Pondicherry was the principal French stronghold.

Q98. [Tough] Which pair correctly identifies a major French leader and an English commander in the Carnatic struggle?
A. Dupleix and Eyre Coote
B. Almeida and Thomas Best
C. Albuquerque and William Hawkins
D. Nino da Cunha and John Surman
ANSWER: A. Dupleix and Eyre Coote
EXPLANATION: Dupleix was a leading French figure; Eyre Coote commanded the English at Wandiwash.

Q99. [Tough] Which combination best explains why the French company struggled to finance its wars?
A. Territorial ambitions weakened commercial returns, while the English retained a stronger commercial base
B. The French had no interest in political influence
C. The English were forbidden to trade
D. The French controlled every major Indian port
ANSWER: A. Territorial ambitions weakened commercial returns, while the English retained a stronger commercial base
EXPLANATION: The PDF says French territorial ambitions drained funds, whereas English commercial interests provided a sound financial base.

Q100. [Tough] Which statement about the Portuguese and European maritime expansion is most accurate according to the PDF?
A. Portuguese naval methods and organisation influenced later European powers, including the English and Dutch
B. Portuguese techniques had no influence on other Europeans
C. Only inland cavalry mattered in maritime competition
D. The Portuguese avoided using ship-mounted guns
ANSWER: A. Portuguese naval methods and organisation influenced later European powers, including the English and Dutch
EXPLANATION: The Portuguese developed naval techniques, artillery, pilot systems and dockyard organisation that other Europeans learned from.

A: A region with heavy rainfall
B: A large, dry area with very little rainfall
C: A region covered with dense forests

D: A polar region
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Tallgrass prairies receive moderate rainfall and have rich, fertile soil. The grass in these regions can grow to more than 5 feet in height.

CLASS: 5
SUBJECT: Geography
CHAPTER: Temperate Climate (The Prairies)
LEVEL: Tough
Q: Why is the mixed grass prairie called an ecotone?
A: It is found only in deserts
B: It contains no grass
C: It is located entirely in the mountains
D: It forms a transition area between tallgrass and short grass prairies
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: An ecotone is a transition area between two biological communities. The mixed grass prairie lies between tallgrass and short grass prairies.

EXPLANATION: In 1917, Gandhi travelled to Champaran in Bihar to support peasants who were suffering under an oppressive plantation system.

CLASS: 10

ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: During Non-Cooperation, people were encouraged to boycott British institutions and goods. Civil Disobedience went further by asking people to break colonial laws, including the salt law, and refuse certain taxes.

D: Poor peasants were not interested in land-related issues
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Poor peasants often rented land from landlords and wanted their unpaid rents to be remitted. The Congress feared that supporting no-rent campaigns would alienate rich peasants and landlords, creating tensions within the movement.

CLASS: 10

ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Rich peasants supported the movement because falling prices reduced their income and made revenue payments difficult. When the movement was called off in 1931 without reducing revenue rates, many were disappointed and some refused to participate when it restarted in 1932.

CLASS: 10

CLASS: 10

CLASS: 10

Q: What limitation of nationalist symbols and historical interpretations does the chapter highlight?

CLASS: 10

CHAPTER: Nationalism in India
LEVEL: Tough
Q: Why did the Civil Disobedience Movement lose momentum by 1934 despite its wide participation?
A: It had achieved every demand made by all social groups

C: The British government immediately granted Purna Swaraj
D: Every social group withdrew from politics before 1930
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The movement faced severe colonial repression and differing expectations among rich peasants, poor peasants, industrialists, workers, women, Dalits, and Muslim political groups. These differences complicated efforts to maintain a united struggle, and the movement lost momentum by 1934.

CLASS: 10
SUBJECT: History
CHAPTER: Nationalism in India
LEVEL: Tough
Q: What broader historical lesson does the chapter draw from the different meanings of Swaraj among Indian social groups?
A: National movements always have identical goals for every participant
B: Economic grievances have no connection with political movements
C: A common national movement can unite diverse groups while their expectations and priorities remain different
D: Only one social group participated in India's national movement
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The chapter shows that peasants, tribals, plantation workers, industrialists, workers, women, Dalits, and other groups participated for different reasons. Their shared opposition to colonial rule created common ground, but their ideas of freedom were not always the same.

CLASS: 10

Q: In which year did the French Revolution begin?
A: 1776
B: 1815
C: 1789
D: 1848
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The French Revolution began in 1789. It challenged absolute monarchy and established the principle that sovereignty belonged to the people rather than the monarch.

CLASS: 10
SUBJECT: History
CHAPTER: The Rise of Nationalism in Europe
LEVEL: Easy

Q: What did the French revolutionaries mean by the term 'la patrie'?
A: The fatherland
B: The royal palace
C: The national army
D: The European empire
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The term 'la patrie' means the fatherland. Together with 'le citoyen', meaning the citizen, it emphasised the idea of a united community enjoying equal rights under a constitution.

CLASS: 10
SUBJECT: History
CHAPTER: The Rise of Nationalism in Europe
LEVEL: Easy

Q: Which flag was adopted by the French revolutionaries to replace the former royal standard?
A: The black, red and gold flag

ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: Napoleon simplified administrative divisions, abolished the feudal system in many territories, removed guild restrictions and improved transport and communication. These changes benefited peasants, artisans, workers and businessmen in different ways.

ANSWER: A

ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Liberalism emphasised individual freedom, equality before the law, government by consent, constitutional government and representative institutions. It also defended the inviolability of private property.

D: Only women were allowed to vote
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Equality before the law did not automatically guarantee equal political rights. In revolutionary France, voting rights were initially restricted to property-owning men, while women and men without property were excluded.

EXPLANATION: The Zollverein was a customs union formed at Prussia's initiative and joined by most German states. It abolished tariff barriers and reduced the number of currencies, helping economic integration and nationalist sentiment.

ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Herder believed that true German culture could be found among the common people, or das Volk. Folk songs, folk poetry and folk dances expressed the spirit of the nation, known as Volksgeist.

D: The Austrian Empire granted independence to all German states
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Silesian weavers faced severe hardship because contractors reduced the prices paid for finished textiles. In June 1845, they protested and attacked the property of a contractor. The army was called in, and eleven weavers were shot.

CLASS: 10

Q: Why did the Balkans become a major source of nationalist tension after 1871?

D: The European powers had agreed to remove their influence from the region

EXPLANATION: The Balkans contained diverse peoples, including Slavic nationalities. As Ottoman control weakened, different groups sought independence and territory. Rivalries among Russia, Germany, Britain and Austria-Hungary made the region a centre of international conflict.

Q: How did the nationalist aspirations of Balkan peoples contribute to international conflict?

C: Balkan nationalists rejected the idea of national identity
D: The Ottoman Empire immediately became a democratic federation
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Balkan nationalities used historical claims to demand independence and political rights. Rivalry between Balkan states over territory, combined with competition among European powers, contributed to wars and ultimately the First World War.

Q: Which statement best describes the relationship between nationalism and imperialism by 1914?

B: Imperialism had no connection with national identity
C: All European powers had accepted the principle of equal independence for every people
D: Nationalism increasingly served imperialist ambitions and contributed to international tensions that culminated in war
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: By the late nineteenth century, nationalist sentiment was increasingly used to justify imperial expansion and competition. Rivalries among European powers, particularly in the Balkans, contributed to the disaster of 1914 and the First World War.

CLASS: 10
SUBJECT: History
CHAPTER: The Rise of Nationalism in Europe
LEVEL: Tough

Q: What common feature connected anti-imperial movements in colonised countries during the nineteenth century?
A: They sought independent nation-states and developed collective unity through opposition to imperial domination
B: They all copied European nationalism in exactly the same form
C: They supported permanent colonial rule
D: They rejected every form of national identity
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Anti-imperial movements aimed to form independent nation-states and developed a sense of collective national unity through resistance to imperialism. The chapter emphasises that different societies developed their own specific forms of nationalism.





CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Earth, Moon, and the Sun
LEVEL: Tough

Q: What is the primary reason why the Sun appears to move from East to West across the sky?
A: The Sun revolves around the Earth every 24 hours
B: The Earth rotates on its axis from West to East
C: The Earth revolves around the Moon
D: The Sun rotates around the Earth's axis
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The Earth rotates on its axis from West to East. Due to this rotation, the Sun appears to rise in the East and set in the West. This is called the apparent motion of the Sun.

Q: Approximately how long does the Earth take to complete one rotation on its axis, according to the chapter?
A: 12 hours
B: 365 days
C: 24 hours
D: 30 days
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The Earth completes one rotation on its axis in approximately 24 hours. This rotation causes the cycle of day and night.

Q: When viewed from above the North Pole, in which direction does the Earth rotate?
A: Anti-clockwise
B: Clockwise
C: From North to South
D: From South to North
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: According to the chapter, the Earth rotates in an anti-clockwise direction when viewed from above the North Pole. Its rotation is from West to East.

Q: Which statement correctly distinguishes rotation from revolution?
A: Rotation means moving around the Sun, while revolution means spinning on an axis
B: Both rotation and revolution describe the same movement
C: Rotation occurs only in stars, while revolution occurs only in planets
D: Rotation is spinning on an axis, while revolution is movement around another object
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: Rotation is the motion of an object around its own axis. Revolution is the motion of an object around another object.

Q: Why does one half of the Earth experience daytime while the other half experiences night at a particular moment?
A: The Moon blocks sunlight from reaching half of the Earth
B: The half facing the Sun receives sunlight, while the opposite half remains in darkness
C: The Earth stops rotating during the night
D: The Sun illuminates only the Northern Hemisphere
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Sunlight illuminates approximately half of the Earth's spherical surface at a time. The side facing the Sun experiences day, while the opposite side experiences night.

Q: What causes sunrise to occur earlier in the eastern parts of India than in its western parts?
A: The eastern parts of India are closer to the Sun
B: The Sun moves faster above eastern India
C: The Earth rotates from West to East
D: The Earth revolves around the Sun from East to West
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Because the Earth rotates from West to East, places farther east enter the illuminated part of the Earth earlier than places farther west.

Q: Which statement about the Earth's axis is correct?
A: It is an imaginary line passing through the North and South Poles
B: It is a physical rod connecting the Earth to the Sun
C: It is the circular path followed by the Moon
D: It is the boundary between day and night
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The Earth's axis is an imaginary line passing through the geographic North Pole and South Pole. The Earth rotates around this axis.

Q: Why does the Pole Star appear nearly stationary in the Northern Hemisphere?
A: It is the brightest star and does not emit light
B: It revolves around the Earth in exactly 24 hours
C: It is located inside the Earth's atmosphere
D: The Earth's axis of rotation points very close to it
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The Earth's axis of rotation points very close to the Pole Star. Therefore, the Pole Star appears nearly stationary while other stars appear to move around it.

Q: What causes the apparent daily movement of stars across the night sky?
A: The stars revolve around the Earth every 24 hours
B: The Earth rotates on its axis
C: The Moon revolves around the Sun
D: The Earth's revolution around the Sun
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The apparent daily movement of stars is caused by the Earth's rotation. The stars appear to move across the sky in the direction opposite to the Earth's rotation.

Q: Which scientist used a long pendulum to provide a simple demonstration of the Earth's rotation?
A: Galileo Galilei
B: Aryabhata
C: Léon Foucault
D: Johannes Kepler
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Léon Foucault used a long pendulum in the nineteenth century to demonstrate the Earth's rotation. Such a pendulum is known as a Foucault pendulum.

Q: According to the chapter, approximately how long does the Earth take to complete one revolution around the Sun?
A: 365 days and 6 hours
B: 24 hours
C: 30 days
D: 12 hours
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The Earth takes approximately 365 days and 6 hours to complete one revolution around the Sun. This period is approximately one year.

Q: What is the path followed by the Earth as it revolves around the Sun called?
A: Axis
B: Equator
C: Hemisphere
D: Orbit
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The path followed by an object while revolving around another object is called its orbit. The Earth's orbit around the Sun is nearly circular when viewed from above.

Q: Why do different stars and constellations become visible in the night sky during different months of the year?
A: The stars disappear permanently during certain months
B: The Earth's revolution around the Sun changes the direction of the night sky we observe
C: The Sun carries all the stars around the Earth
D: The Earth's rotation stops during particular months
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: As the Earth revolves around the Sun, the direction in which the night side faces in space changes. Consequently, different stars and constellations become visible during different months.

Q: Which statement correctly explains why seasons occur on Earth?
A: The Earth moves closer to the Sun every summer in both hemispheres
B: The Sun produces more heat in June than in December
C: The tilt of the Earth's axis and the spherical shape of the Earth cause seasons
D: The Moon blocks sunlight during winter
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Seasons occur because the Earth's axis is tilted relative to its orbit and because the Earth is spherical. These factors influence the intensity of sunlight and the duration of daytime in different hemispheres.

Q: What happens in the Northern Hemisphere during June?
A: It is tilted towards the Sun and experiences summer
B: It is tilted away from the Sun and experiences winter
C: It receives no sunlight
D: It experiences the shortest day of the year
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: In June, the Northern Hemisphere is tilted towards the Sun. It receives more intense sunlight and generally has longer days, producing summer conditions.

Q: What is the main reason the Northern Hemisphere experiences winter in December?
A: The Earth stops revolving around the Sun
B: The Earth is at its greatest possible distance from the Sun
C: The Sun stops producing heat
D: The Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun and receives less intense sunlight for a shorter duration
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: In December, the Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun. It receives less intense sunlight and has shorter days, resulting in winter.

Q: Which statement correctly describes the seasons in the Southern Hemisphere during June?
A: It experiences summer because it is tilted towards the Sun
B: It experiences winter while the Northern Hemisphere experiences summer
C: It experiences no season because it receives no sunlight
D: It experiences spring everywhere
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The seasons in the Southern Hemisphere are opposite to those in the Northern Hemisphere. Therefore, when the Northern Hemisphere experiences summer in June, the Southern Hemisphere experiences winter.

Q: Why is the explanation that summer occurs because the Earth is closer to the Sun incorrect?
A: The Earth does not revolve around the Sun
B: The Earth is always at the same distance from the Sun
C: Seasons are primarily caused by axial tilt and the distribution of sunlight, not small distance differences
D: The Sun is closer to the Southern Hemisphere throughout the year
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The chapter explains that seasons are caused by the Earth's axial tilt and spherical shape. The Earth is actually closest to the Sun in January, demonstrating that distance alone does not explain the seasons.

Q: Around which date does the longest day occur in the Northern Hemisphere?
A: Around 21 June
B: Around 22 December
C: Around 21 March
D: Around 23 September
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The longest day in the Northern Hemisphere occurs around 21 June. This date is known as the summer solstice.

Q: What is the winter solstice in the Northern Hemisphere?
A: The day when the Earth completes one revolution
B: The day when the Sun disappears permanently
C: The day when daytime and nighttime are always equal
D: The day around 22 December when the shortest day and longest night occur
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: Around 22 December, the Northern Hemisphere experiences its shortest day and longest night. This is called the winter solstice.

Q: What happens to the duration of daylight around 21 March and 23 September in the Northern Hemisphere?
A: Daylight lasts for 24 hours
B: Day and night are approximately equal, lasting 12 hours each
C: Night lasts for 18 hours
D: Daylight lasts for only 6 hours
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Around 21 March and 23 September, daytime lasts approximately 12 hours. These dates are associated with the spring equinox and autumn equinox, respectively, in the Northern Hemisphere.

Q: Which statement about the equator is given in the chapter?
A: It experiences six months of darkness every year
B: It has winter in June and summer in December
C: It generally receives about 12 hours of sunlight and 12 hours of darkness throughout the year
D: It receives no direct sunlight
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The chapter states that the equator generally experiences approximately 12 hours of sunlight and 12 hours of darkness. Seasonal differences in the intensity of sunlight there are relatively small.

Q: What happens at the North Pole around the June solstice, according to the chapter?
A: It receives sunlight for all 24 hours
B: It experiences darkness for all 24 hours
C: The Sun rises and sets every 12 hours
D: It receives no sunlight for six months
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Around the June solstice, the North Pole receives sunlight continuously for 24 hours. This is a result of the Earth's axial tilt.

Q: What is a solar eclipse?
A: An event in which the Earth blocks sunlight from reaching the Moon
B: An event in which the Sun moves behind another star
C: An event in which the Earth comes between the Sun and the Moon
D: An event in which the Moon comes between the Sun and the Earth, blocking sunlight from reaching the Earth
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: A solar eclipse occurs when the Moon comes between the Sun and the Earth in such a way that it blocks some or all of the Sun's light from reaching an observer on Earth.

Q: Why can the Moon cover the Sun during a total solar eclipse even though it is much smaller than the Sun?
A: The Moon produces more light than the Sun
B: The Moon is much closer to Earth, so its apparent size can be similar to the Sun's
C: The Sun becomes physically smaller during an eclipse
D: The Earth magnifies the Moon during an eclipse
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Apparent size depends on both an object's actual size and its distance from the observer. Although the Moon is much smaller than the Sun, it is also much closer to Earth, allowing it to cover the Sun from our viewpoint.

Q: What is a total solar eclipse?
A: An event in which the Earth completely blocks the Moon
B: An event in which the Moon disappears permanently
C: An event in which the Moon completely covers the Sun as seen from a particular area on Earth
D: An event in which the Sun stops emitting light
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: During a total solar eclipse, the Moon completely blocks the Sun from the viewpoint of observers within a limited area on Earth. Those observers cannot see any part of the Sun's bright disc.

Q: What is a partial solar eclipse?
A: An event in which the Moon blocks only part of the Sun as seen by an observer
B: An event in which the Earth blocks the entire Sun
C: An event in which the Moon blocks the entire Earth
D: An event in which the Sun blocks the Moon
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: A partial solar eclipse occurs when the Moon covers only a portion of the Sun's visible disc. Some part of the Sun remains visible to the observer.

Q: Why is a total solar eclipse visible only for a few minutes at a particular location?
A: The Sun stops shining after a few minutes
B: The Earth stops rotating during the eclipse
C: The Moon permanently leaves its orbit
D: The Moon's shadow moves across the Earth's surface because of the Earth's rotation and the Moon's orbital motion
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The Moon's shadow moves across the Earth due to the Earth's rotation and the Moon's motion in its orbit. Therefore, totality lasts only a short time at a particular location.

Q: Which statement about safely observing a solar eclipse is correct?
A: It is safe to look directly at the Sun during totality at every stage of an eclipse
B: Direct viewing of a solar eclipse must be avoided; specialised solar-viewing protection or safe projection methods should be used
C: Ordinary sunglasses provide complete protection
D: Binoculars can be used without solar filters
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The chapter warns that looking directly at the Sun during a solar eclipse can damage the eyes and cause blindness. Ordinary sunglasses, binoculars, and telescopes without proper solar protection must not be used for direct viewing.

Q: What is a lunar eclipse?
A: An event in which the Moon comes between the Sun and the Earth
B: An event in which Venus blocks the Sun
C: An event in which the Earth comes between the Sun and the Moon, blocking sunlight from reaching the Moon
D: An event in which the Sun revolves around the Moon
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: A lunar eclipse occurs when the Earth comes between the Sun and the Moon, causing the Earth's shadow to fall on the Moon.

Q: What is a total lunar eclipse?
A: An event in which the Moon is completely within the Earth's shadow
B: An event in which the Moon completely covers the Sun
C: An event in which the Earth enters the Moon's orbit permanently
D: An event in which the Moon stops reflecting sunlight forever
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: During a total lunar eclipse, the Moon is completely within the Earth's shadow. The Moon's bright disc appears dark red during this event, as described in the chapter.

Q: How does the appearance of the Moon change during a total lunar eclipse, according to the chapter?
A: It becomes permanently invisible
B: It appears bright blue
C: It becomes larger than the Sun
D: Its bright disc appears dark red
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: During a total lunar eclipse, the Moon's bright disc appears dark red while it remains within the Earth's shadow. It returns to its usual appearance as it moves out of the shadow.

Q: Which statement correctly compares solar and lunar eclipses?
A: Both occur when the Sun comes between the Earth and the Moon
B: A solar eclipse involves the Moon blocking sunlight from reaching Earth, while a lunar eclipse involves Earth's shadow falling on the Moon
C: A solar eclipse occurs every day, while a lunar eclipse never occurs
D: A lunar eclipse occurs when the Moon blocks the Sun from reaching Earth
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: During a solar eclipse, the Moon is between the Sun and the Earth. During a lunar eclipse, the Earth is between the Sun and the Moon.

Q: Why can a lunar eclipse be observed from a larger part of Earth than a total solar eclipse?
A: The Moon is larger than the Earth
B: The Sun illuminates only the lunar surface during an eclipse
C: The Earth's shadow on the Moon can be seen from a much larger region on Earth's night side, while the Moon's shadow covers only a limited area during a total solar eclipse
D: Solar eclipses occur only over oceans
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: A lunar eclipse can be observed from many locations on the Earth's night side where the Moon is visible. A total solar eclipse is restricted to a relatively narrow region through which the Moon's shadow passes.

Q: Which of the following correctly represents the arrangement during a solar eclipse?
A: Sun – Moon – Earth
B: Moon – Sun – Earth
C: Earth – Moon – Sun
D: Sun – Earth – Moon
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: During a solar eclipse, the Moon comes between the Sun and the Earth. The alignment is Sun–Moon–Earth.

Q: Which of the following correctly represents the arrangement during a lunar eclipse?
A: Moon – Sun – Earth
B: Earth – Moon – Sun
C: Moon – Earth – Sun
D: Sun – Earth – Moon
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: During a lunar eclipse, the Earth comes between the Sun and the Moon. The alignment is Sun–Earth–Moon.

Q: Why can Venus not block the entire Sun as viewed from Earth when it passes between the Sun and Earth?
A: Venus is smaller than every other planet
B: Venus appears much smaller than the Sun because it is much farther away from Earth than the Moon
C: Venus does not revolve around the Sun
D: Venus produces no reflected light
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The chapter explains that although Venus is physically larger than the Moon, it is much farther from Earth. Consequently, Venus appears as a tiny black dot against the Sun rather than covering its entire disc.

Q: What is a Transit of Venus?
A: The movement of Venus around the Moon
B: A type of lunar eclipse
C: The appearance of Venus as a tiny black dot passing across the bright face of the Sun
D: The disappearance of Venus behind the Earth
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: A Transit of Venus occurs when Venus passes between the Sun and Earth and appears as a tiny black dot against the Sun's bright face. The chapter describes this as a rare event.

Q: Which ancient Indian astronomer wrote the Aryabhatiya around the fifth century CE?
A: Aryabhata
B: Varahamihira
C: Brahmagupta
D: Bhaskara II
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Aryabhata was a famous ancient Indian mathematician and astronomer. He wrote the Aryabhatiya around the fifth century CE and explained the apparent motion of stars due to Earth's rotation.

Q: What value for the Earth's rotational period did Aryabhata state, according to the chapter?
A: Exactly 365 days
B: Exactly 30 days
C: Exactly 12 hours
D: Approximately 23 hours, 56 minutes, and 4.1 seconds
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The chapter notes that Aryabhata's stated value for one complete rotation was approximately 23 hours, 56 minutes, and 4.1 seconds in modern units.

Q: Which Indian observatory, established in 1899, has provided data about the Sun for over 100 years?
A: Jantar Mantar, Jaipur
B: Kodaikanal Solar Observatory
C: India Meteorological Observatory, Delhi
D: Mount Abu Observatory
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The Kodaikanal Solar Observatory was established in 1899 in the Palani range of hills in southern India. It has provided solar data for more than 100 years.

Q: Who is known as the father of modern Indian astronomy, according to the chapter?
A: Aryabhata
B: C. V. Raman
C: M. K. Vainu Bappu
D: Satyendra Nath Bose
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: M. K. Vainu Bappu is known as the father of modern Indian astronomy. He contributed to establishing astronomical instruments and telescopes in India and studied stars and solar eclipses.

Q: Which statement about the Earth's seasons is scientifically consistent with the chapter?
A: Seasons are caused by the tilt of the Earth's axis and its spherical shape
B: Seasons occur because the Earth stops rotating four times a year
C: Seasons are caused entirely by changes in the Sun's temperature
D: Seasons occur because the Moon blocks sunlight every winter
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The chapter identifies the tilt of the Earth's axis and its spherical shape as the reasons for seasons. These factors change the intensity and duration of sunlight received by different hemispheres.

Q: If the Earth's axis were not tilted with respect to its orbit, what would most likely happen to the seasonal pattern?
A: The Northern Hemisphere would experience six months of complete darkness every year
B: The Earth would stop revolving around the Sun
C: The Moon would no longer revolve around the Earth
D: The usual seasonal differences caused by axial tilt would be greatly reduced or absent
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The chapter explains that seasons occur because the Earth's axis is tilted. Without this tilt, the usual seasonal changes caused by variations in sunlight intensity and day length would be greatly reduced or absent.

Q: Which statement best explains why summer days are generally longer in the Northern Hemisphere around June?
A: The Earth rotates more slowly in June
B: The Northern Hemisphere is tilted towards the Sun and remains illuminated for more than 12 hours at many locations
C: The Sun moves closer to the Northern Hemisphere every morning
D: The Moon reflects extra sunlight towards the Northern Hemisphere
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: During June, the Northern Hemisphere is tilted towards the Sun. Many places in this hemisphere receive sunlight for more than 12 hours during a 24-hour rotation, contributing to summer.

Q: What would happen if the Earth did not rotate on its axis but continued revolving around the Sun?
A: The ordinary 24-hour cycle of day and night caused by rotation would no longer occur
B: The Moon would immediately stop revolving
C: The Earth would no longer have an orbit
D: The Sun would stop emitting light
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The daily cycle of day and night is caused by Earth's rotation. If the Earth stopped rotating, this ordinary rotation-driven day-night cycle would no longer occur, even if the Earth continued to revolve around the Sun.

Q: Which observation supports the conclusion that the Earth rotates on its axis?
A: The Earth completes one revolution around the Sun in approximately a year
B: The Moon has phases
C: The Sun appears to move across the sky from East to West, and stars appear to move during the night
D: The planets have different sizes
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The apparent daily movement of the Sun, Moon, and stars is explained by the Earth's rotation from West to East. The chapter also describes the Foucault pendulum as a demonstration of Earth's rotation.

Q: Which statement about the movement of the Moon across the sky is correct?
A: The Moon appears to rise in the East and set in the West because of Earth's rotation
B: The Moon always rises in the West
C: The Moon never appears to move across the sky
D: The Moon's daily apparent motion proves that it revolves around the Sun every 24 hours
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Like the Sun and stars, the Moon appears to rise in the East and set in the West because the Earth rotates from West to East. The Moon also revolves around the Earth.

Q: Which statement best explains the relationship between Earth's revolution and the changing night sky?
A: Earth's revolution causes all stars to disappear permanently
B: Earth's revolution changes the position of the Pole Star every hour
C: Earth's revolution makes the Moon produce its own light
D: As Earth revolves around the Sun, the night side faces different directions in space during the year
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: Earth's revolution changes the direction in space towards which the night side faces. Therefore, different stars and constellations become visible after sunset at different times of the year.

Q: A student claims that winter occurs in December because the Earth is farthest from the Sun. Which response best matches the chapter?
A: The claim is correct because the Earth is farthest from the Sun in December
B: The claim is incorrect because the Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun in December; the Earth is closest to the Sun in January
C: The claim is correct because the Moon blocks the Sun every December
D: The claim is incorrect because the Earth does not revolve around the Sun
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The chapter states that the Earth is closest to the Sun in January. Northern Hemisphere winter occurs because that hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun and receives less intense sunlight for a shorter duration.

Q: Which of the following is the safest way to observe a solar eclipse mentioned in the chapter?
A: Looking directly at the Sun for only a few seconds
B: Using ordinary dark sunglasses
C: Attending an organised eclipse-viewing event with specialised eye protection or using a safe projection method
D: Looking through binoculars without a solar filter
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The chapter recommends organised eclipse-viewing events that provide specialised eye protection and scientific explanations. It also describes projecting the Sun's image onto a screen under appropriate supervision.

Q: What is the main difference between the shadow observed during a solar eclipse and the shadow observed during a lunar eclipse?
A: During a solar eclipse, the Moon's shadow falls on Earth; during a lunar eclipse, Earth's shadow falls on the Moon
B: During both eclipses, the Sun's shadow falls on the Moon
C: During a solar eclipse, Earth's shadow falls on the Sun
D: During a lunar eclipse, the Moon's shadow falls on the Sun
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: During a solar eclipse, the Moon blocks sunlight and casts its shadow on Earth. During a lunar eclipse, Earth blocks sunlight from reaching the Moon, and Earth's shadow falls on the Moon.

Q: Why does a total solar eclipse affect only a limited region of the Earth's surface at a particular time?
A: The Earth becomes smaller during an eclipse
B: The Sun illuminates only one continent
C: The Moon blocks sunlight from every part of Earth simultaneously
D: The Moon's shadow covers only a relatively small area of Earth's surface
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The Moon's shadow covers a limited region of the Earth's surface. Observers within that region may experience a total solar eclipse, while observers elsewhere may see a partial eclipse or no eclipse.

Q: Which statement about the Foucault pendulum in India's new Parliament building is mentioned in the chapter?
A: It is a 22-metre-long pendulum that symbolises the integration of India with the vastness of the cosmos
B: It measures the distance between Earth and the Moon
C: It measures the temperature of the Sun
D: It proves that the Earth revolves around the Moon
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: The chapter mentions a Foucault pendulum with a length of 22 metres in the Constitution Hall of the new Parliament building in New Delhi. It symbolises the integration of the idea of India with the vastness of the cosmos.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Exploring Substances: Acidic, Basic, and Neutral
LEVEL: Tough
Q: Which acid is identified in the chapter as being present in vinegar?
A: Citric acid
B: Tartaric acid
C: Acetic acid
D: Ascorbic acid

EXPLANATION: An LED allows current to pass through it in only one direction. Its positive terminal must connect to the battery's positive terminal and its negative terminal to the battery's negative terminal for it to glow as described in the chapter.

Q: Which statement correctly distinguishes a physical change from a chemical change?

B: A chemical change only changes the shape of a substance

D: A chemical change is always reversible
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: A physical change affects properties such as shape, size, or state without forming a new substance. A chemical change involves the formation of one or more new substances.

C: The paper undergoes combustion

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Heat Transfer in Nature
LEVEL: Tough
Q: What is conduction of heat?
A: Heat transfer through the actual movement of particles from one place to another
B: Heat transfer that occurs only through empty space
C: Heat transfer from the hotter part of an object to the colder part without the particles moving from their positions
D: Heat transfer that occurs only when a substance changes its state
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Conduction is the transfer of heat from the hotter part of an object to the colder part. Particles transfer heat to neighbouring particles without moving away from their positions.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Heat Transfer in Nature
LEVEL: Tough

B: The pin nearest to the flame is always lighter
C: The metal strip contracts immediately when heated
D: The candle produces a force that pulls the nearest pin down
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Heat travels from the heated end of the metal strip towards its colder end. The wax holding the nearest pin melts first, causing that pin to fall before the others.

D: Heat gradually travels along the strip, melting the wax at different positions at different times
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: Heat travels from the hotter end towards the colder end of the metal strip. The wax melts first near the heated end and later at positions farther away, causing the pins to fall in sequence.

Q: Which statement correctly distinguishes conduction from convection?

EXPLANATION: In conduction, particles transfer heat to neighbouring particles without moving away from their positions. In convection, heat is transferred by the actual movement of particles in fluids such as liquids and gases.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Measurement of Time and Motion
LEVEL: Tough
Q: When is a pendulum said to have completed one oscillation if it starts from its mean position O?
A: When it moves from O to extreme position A only
B: When it moves from O to A and stops permanently
C: When it moves from O to B without changing direction
D: When it moves from O to A, then to B, and finally returns to O
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: One complete oscillation occurs when the bob starts from its mean position, moves to one extreme, then to the other extreme, and returns to the mean position. The bob has then completed one full cycle of motion.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Measurement of Time and Motion
LEVEL: Tough
Q: A pendulum bob starts at extreme position A, moves to extreme position B, and returns to A. How many oscillations has it completed?
A: One complete oscillation
B: Half an oscillation
C: Two complete oscillations
D: One-quarter of an oscillation
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Moving from one extreme position to the other and returning to the starting extreme position constitutes one complete oscillation. Therefore, the motion from A to B and back to A is one oscillation.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Measurement of Time and Motion
LEVEL: Tough
Q: What is the time period of a simple pendulum?
A: The total distance travelled by its bob
B: The time taken to complete one oscillation
C: The maximum distance between its extreme positions
D: The number of oscillations completed in one hour
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: The time period is the time taken by a pendulum to complete one oscillation. It is commonly measured in seconds and can be determined by timing several oscillations and dividing the total time by their number.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Measurement of Time and Motion
LEVEL: Tough
Q: A pendulum completes 10 oscillations in 18 seconds. What is its time period?
A: 18 s
B: 0.18 s
C: 1.8 s
D: 180 s
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Time period = Total time taken ÷ Number of oscillations. Therefore, time period = 18 s ÷ 10 = 1.8 s.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Measurement of Time and Motion
LEVEL: Tough
Q: A student measures the time for 10 oscillations of a pendulum as 20.4 s, 20.1 s, and 20.5 s in three trials. What is the average time period calculated from the combined observations?
A: Approximately 2.03 s
B: Approximately 20.33 s
C: Approximately 6.77 s
D: Approximately 203 s
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: Total time for three trials = 20.4 + 20.1 + 20.5 = 61.0 s. The average time for 10 oscillations is 61.0 ÷ 3 = 20.33 s. Dividing by 10 gives an average time period of approximately 2.03 s.

ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: The PDF states that the time period of a simple pendulum depends on its length but not on the bob's mass. Pendulums of the same length at a given location have the same time period.

Q: A vehicle travels 60 km in the first hour, 70 km in the second hour, and 50 km in the third hour. Which statement is correct?

B: The vehicle's speed remains constant at 70 km/h
C: The motion is non-uniform, and the average speed is 60 km/h
D: The motion is uniform, and the average speed is 50 km/h
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: The vehicle covers unequal distances in equal one-hour intervals, so its motion is non-uniform. Total distance = 60 + 70 + 50 = 180 km. Total time = 3 h. Average speed = 180 ÷ 3 = 60 km/h.

Q: Which condition defines uniform linear motion?

B: An object changes its direction continuously
C: An object covers unequal distances in equal time intervals
D: An object remains at rest forever
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: An object is in uniform linear motion when it moves along a straight line at constant speed. It covers equal distances in equal intervals of time.

D: An object's speed changes while it moves along a straight line
ANSWER: D
EXPLANATION: Non-uniform linear motion occurs when an object moving along a straight line has a changing speed. Such an object generally covers unequal distances in equal intervals of time.

CLASS: 7
SUBJECT: Science
CHAPTER: Measurement of Time and Motion
LEVEL: Tough
Q: A vehicle travels 2 km. It covers the first 500 m at 10 m/s and the next 500 m at 5 m/s. It must complete the entire journey in 200 seconds. What speed is required for the remaining 1 km?
A: 5 m/s
B: 6 m/s
C: 8 m/s
D: 10 m/s
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Time for the first 500 m = 500 ÷ 10 = 50 s. Time for the next 500 m = 500 ÷ 5 = 100 s. Time already used = 150 s. Remaining time = 200 − 150 = 50 s. Required speed for the remaining 1000 m = 1000 ÷ 50 = 20 m/s. Therefore, none of the listed options is correct; the correct answer should be 20 m/s.

D: Blood, bile, and carbon dioxide

EXPLANATION: The stomach lining secretes digestive juice, acid, and mucus. The digestive juice helps break down proteins, acid helps in digestion and kills many harmful bacteria, and mucus protects the stomach lining.

D: The alveoli stop exchanging gases permanently
ANSWER: A
EXPLANATION: During exhalation, the ribs move down and inward while the diaphragm moves upward. This reduces the space inside the chest and pushes air out of the lungs.

Q: Which pairing of plant transport tissues and their functions is correct?
A: Xylem transports food; phloem transports water only
B: Xylem transports water and minerals; phloem transports food
C: Xylem transports oxygen; phloem transports carbon dioxide only
D: Xylem stores starch; phloem absorbs sunlight
ANSWER: B
EXPLANATION: Xylem transports water and minerals from the roots to other parts of the plant. Phloem transports food prepared in the leaves to other parts of the plant.

D: In the potato only by absorbing sunlight directly
ANSWER: C
EXPLANATION: Leaves are the primary sites of photosynthesis. The food prepared there is transported through the phloem and may be stored in other plant parts, including roots and tubers such as potatoes.

B: Salivary amylase

D: Lipase

D: Flatworms
